# Experiments

Each experiment states the question, the hypothesis with a number attached, the exact procedure, what counts as pass, and where the result lands. Negative results are results. All output JSON goes to `results/` (git-ignored) and the summarised numbers move into `docs/BENCHMARKS.md` by hand with the JSON committed under `evidence/` once reviewed for identifiers.

Legend: **GPU-free** experiments do not touch the cards but still need a quiet host (no GLM benchmark in flight) for CPU/DRAM/NVMe numbers to mean anything. **GPU** experiments need `docker stop glm53-flash-2x-rtxpro6000` first and `docker start` afterwards. Torch-based GPU-free benches run as `docker run --rm -v "$PWD:/w" -w /w --entrypoint python3 dsv4-nvfp4:recipe scripts/<bench>.py` (no `--gpus`).

## E1 — Host DRAM bandwidth (GPU-free)

- Question: how much of the 409.6 GB/s theoretical does the 16-core 9955WX reach?
- Hypothesis: 250–320 GB/s copy bandwidth with 16–32 workers; a single worker ≈ 20–30 GB/s.
- Procedure: `scripts/bench-dram.py --workers 1,2,4,8,16,32 --size-gib 8 --output results/e1.json` (stdlib `mmap` memcpy via `memoryview` slicing in N *processes*; threads hold the GIL and measure one core). STREAM inside a container is the optional follow-up.
- Pass: a measured number replaces the "estimate" row in HARDWARE.md. There is no fail.
- Feeds: the hybrid decode ceiling (`feasibility-calc.py --dram-gbps`); Engram-in-DRAM prefill estimate.
- **Result (run):** 60.0 GB/s single process, 139.9 GB/s at 16, 146.2 GB/s at 32 (read+write). Below hypothesis; a Python lower bound. **This is now the number the 2-GPU verdict's ceiling rests on** (56 tok/s at 146; 89 at 300). Follow-up E1b: a C/STREAM or `torch` CPU read benchmark in the `dsv4-nvfp4:recipe` container, GPU-free, to find the real read bandwidth. [`evidence/e1-dram-bandwidth.json`](../evidence/e1-dram-bandwidth.json).
- **E1b result (run, quiet host, `scripts/bench-dram-read.py` in `dsv4-nvfp4:recipe`, 8 GiB fp32/int64 tensors):** read-only `sum` 67 / **124.7** / 118 GB/s at 8 / 16 / 32 threads; `copy_` (read+write counted) 60.5 / 112.5 / 108.7. Torch's OpenMP reduction is a fair STREAM stand-in here, so **≈125 GB/s read is this CPU's practical DRAM bandwidth** — 30 % of the 409.6 GB/s the eight DDR5-6400 channels can deliver. The E1 146 GB/s "copy" figure counted both directions and is not a read number. The gap is on the CPU side, not the DIMMs: the 9955WX has 16 cores on **two CCDs**, and each CCD's link to the IO die carries roughly 64 B/clk of reads, so two CCDs cannot draw more than ~120–130 GB/s no matter how fast the DIMMs are (hypothesis from the CCD-count arithmetic; E4b below is the confirmation that the DIMMs have headroom). Upgrade path for the hybrid is therefore **more CCDs (9975WX+ / 9985WX), not faster memory**. `--dram-gbps` default is now 125. [`evidence/e1b-dram-read-bandwidth.json`](../evidence/e1b-dram-read-bandwidth.json).

## E2 — GPU memory bandwidth (GPU)

- Question: achieved GDDR7 bandwidth per RTX PRO 6000 Max-Q at 300 W.
- Hypothesis: 1,450–1,650 GB/s device-to-device copy; well below 1,792 GB/s theoretical.
- Procedure: `scripts/bench-pcie-h2d.py --d2d` inside any torch-bearing image on this host (e.g. the `dsv4-sm120` images), 1–8 GiB buffers, 20 iterations, median.
- Pass: number recorded. Used for the decode roofline on the 4-GPU profile (weights per GPU ÷ bandwidth ≈ per-token floor).
- **Result (run, `bench-pcie-h2d.py --d2d`, 256 MiB–4 GiB, 300 W cap):** D2D copy ≈ 1,370 GiB/s = **≈1,470 GB/s** per card counting read+write, i.e. 82 % of the 1,792 GB/s derived from the memory clock — inside the 1,450–1,650 hypothesis. This is the weight-streaming roofline for anything GPU-resident: at decode a kernel that reads 1 GiB of expert weights per token per GPU cannot go faster than ~0.73 ms. E13 stage 1 reaches 1,370–1,400 GB/s of weight bytes at M=1, ≈93 % of this number. [`evidence/e2-e4-gpu-pcie-bandwidth.json`](../evidence/e2-e4-gpu-pcie-bandwidth.json).

## E3 — Engram-style random row gather: DRAM vs NVMe (GPU-free)

- Question: can either drive or DRAM serve Engram lookups at decode and prefill rates?
- Hypothesis: DRAM ≥ 5 M rows/s single-process; Samsung 9100 PRO ≥ 600K random 4 KiB reads/s with O_DIRECT at QD 32 from Python threads (well below the 2.2 M IOPS spec because of Python overhead; document that as a lower bound); T705 lower.
- Procedure: `scripts/bench-engram-gather.py --backend dram --rows 20000000 --output results/e3-dram.json`; `--backend nvme --path results/engram-nvme0.bin` (nvme0, root fs) and `--path /mnt/data/dsv41-engram-bench.bin` (nvme1). Synthetic 20 M × 264 B table (4.9 GiB); no model download. Report rows/s, p50/p99 latency per 48-row token batch.
- Pass: decode batch (48 rows) p99 < 1 ms on both backends; prefill 3.1 M rows in < 10 s on DRAM. Failure on NVMe means Option A must use Engram-in-DRAM mode, which this host can.
- Feeds: HARDWARE.md storage section; choice of `OFFLOAD_MODE` in the 4-GPU profile.
- **Result (run):** DRAM 4.15 M rows/s, batch p99 0.015 ms (re-measured 2026-10-11 copying whole rows: 3.15 M rows/s = 0.83 GB/s, p99 0.020 ms; the original touched 2 bytes per row, R20; conclusions unchanged), 64K-prefill equivalent 0.75 s — pass. Samsung 9100 PRO 403K rows/s, batch p50 1.90 ms; Crucial T705 347K rows/s, p50 2.20 ms — the `< 1 ms` criterion **fails as measured**, but the harness issues a token's 48 reads serially, so the batch latency is 48 × the drive's ≈ 40–46 µs unloaded read latency, not a queueing limit. Corrected reading: both drives sustain decode-rate lookups (≥ 7K token-equivalents/s); NVMe costs ≈ 7.7–8.9 s of lookups per 64K-token prefill versus 0.75 s in DRAM. Decision: the 4-GPU profile keeps `OFFLOAD_MODE=engram-host` (DRAM); NVMe is the fallback if DRAM is needed for something else. Evidence: [`e3-engram-gather-dram.json`](../evidence/e3-engram-gather-dram.json), [`e3-engram-gather-nvme0.json`](../evidence/e3-engram-gather-nvme0.json), [`e3-engram-gather-nvme1.json`](../evidence/e3-engram-gather-nvme1.json).

## E4 — PCIe host-to-device bandwidth per GPU (GPU)

- Question: sustained pinned H2D and D2H bandwidth per card, and both cards concurrently.
- Hypothesis: 48–55 GiB/s per card pinned; both concurrently ≥ 90 GiB/s aggregate (separate x16 links); pageable ≈ half.
- Procedure: `scripts/bench-pcie-h2d.py --sizes 256M,1G,4G --pinned --both` inside a torch image.
- Pass: numbers recorded. Sets the PCIe-streaming fallback ceiling (decode tok/s ≤ 1000 / (5 ms + 1.75 GiB ÷ aggregate GiB/s)) and the layerwise-prefill cost of the hybrid (112 GiB of CPU experts per 4,096-token chunk).
- **Result (run):** pinned H2D **53.1 / 52.9 GiB/s** (57 GB/s) per card, D2H 53.3 / 52.4; both cards concurrently **106.1 GiB/s = 114 GB/s aggregate** — the two x16 links are independent (hypothesis met). PCIe-streaming fallback for the 2-GPU hybrid: 1.75 GiB ÷ 114 GB/s = 16.5 ms + 5 ms → **~46 tok/s**, now within 10 % of the CPU-compute figure (50 tok/s at 125 GB/s) because the CPU's own DRAM read rate turned out to be so low. `--pcie-gbps-per-gpu` default is now 57. Layerwise prefill: 112 GiB per 4,096-token chunk ÷ 106 GiB/s ≈ 1.06 s. [`evidence/e2-e4-gpu-pcie-bandwidth.json`](../evidence/e2-e4-gpu-pcie-bandwidth.json).

## E4b — Do CPU reads and GPU DMA reads of host DRAM contend? (GPU)

- Question: E1b shows the CPU cores reach only 125 GB/s of the DIMMs' 410 GB/s, and E4 shows GPU DMA pulls 114 GB/s from the same DRAM. Are those two paths sharing one bottleneck (DIMMs/controller) or two separate ones (CCD fabric vs PCIe root)?
- Hypothesis: separate. The CCD→IOD links cap the cores; DMA enters at the IO die and never crosses them, so running both at once should sum to ≈240 GB/s.
- Procedure: `scripts/bench-dram-contention.py` inside `dsv4-nvfp4:recipe --gpus all`: 8 s phases of (1) 16-thread torch fp32 `sum` over 8 GiB, (2) pinned 4 GiB H2D loop on both GPUs, (3) both at once, (4) CPU-only again; report GB/s per side and additivity = both ÷ (sum of solos).
- Pass: additivity ≥ 0.9 means the hybrid can serve DRAM-resident experts from **both** paths at once (CPU compute + PCIe streaming), and the calculator gets a `split ceiling`.
- **Result (run):** CPU alone 123.7 GB/s; H2D alone 114.0 GB/s (57.0 per GPU); **both at once 120.1 + 113.8 = 233.9 GB/s, additivity 0.984**; CPU-only repeat 123.5 (no drift). Hypothesis confirmed: the CPU-side limit is fabric, the DIMMs have ≥ 110 GB/s of headroom that only the GPUs can reach. Design consequence (Option B2 in FEASIBILITY.md): split the 42 % DRAM-resident experts between CPU compute and per-step PCIe streaming to the GPU in proportion to 125 : 114; calculator ceiling **78 tok/s** at the 125 GB/s read figure, **71 tok/s** at the 94 GB/s the CPU actually sustained while computing in E6 s0. Second consequence: the streamed experts run on the GPU through the E13 kernel, so only ~half of the CPU-side experts need a CPU MXFP4 kernel at all. [`evidence/e4b-dram-contention-cpu-vs-dma.json`](../evidence/e4b-dram-contention-cpu-vs-dma.json).

## E5 — V4.1-Flash on 2 GPUs with generic CPU offload (GPU, needs 475 GiB download)

- Question: what exactly happens when the two available engines are asked to do it?
- Hypothesis: SGLang `dev-dsv41` with `SGLANG_ENABLE_DSV41_ENGRAM_HOST_TABLE=1 --tp 2` OOMs during weight load at ~140 GiB/GPU demand. vLLM `--cpu-offload-gb 110` either fails to construct the deepseek_v41 loader on SM120 or produces < 5 tok/s because it streams all offloaded parameters every step.
- Procedure: `scripts/download-model.sh` (pinned), then `ALLOW_UNQUALIFIED=1 scripts/serve.sh --profile 2gpu-attempt` with each engine; capture the log tail (scrubbed) into `results/e5-*.log`.
- Pass criterion is documentation: the failure mode is recorded verbatim in TROUBLESHOOTING.md. If it unexpectedly serves, run `verify.py` and `bench-decode.py` and treat the number as the new 2-GPU result.

## E6 — Three-tier hybrid: CPU-computed experts (stage 0 GPU-free; stages 1–2 GPU)

- Question: can 42% of V4.1-Flash's routed experts run on this host's CPU from DRAM at a rate that makes the 2-GPU layout useful, and can an engine be made to do it for `deepseek_v41`?
- Hypothesis: decode 40–60 tok/s at batch 1 with 58% of experts on GPU (calculator ceiling 56 tok/s at 146 GB/s); CPU compute ≤ DRAM time when overlapped; prefill 2–3K tok/s with layerwise GPU prefill.
- **Stage 0 — CPU expert GEMV (GPU-free, runnable now).** `scripts/bench-cpu-expert.py` inside `dsv4-nvfp4:recipe` without `--gpus`: a synthetic pool of experts (3 × 5120×2304 matrices each) larger than L3, 101 random experts per token, gate/up/down GEMVs at batch 1, 16 and 32 threads, weights in bf16 (memory roofline) and int8-dequant (compute proxy for MXFP4). Report ms/token and effective GB/s. Pass: a measured CPU ms/token to put next to the 12.9 ms DRAM figure; if bf16 GEMV already exceeds 25 ms for 101 experts, CPU compute — not DRAM — is the bound and the plan changes to PCIe streaming or a 3rd GPU.
  - **Stage 0 result (run, quiet host, pool 256 experts = 12 GiB bf16, 20 tokens × 101 experts):** bf16 GEMV **76.1 ms/token at 16 threads = 93.9 GB/s effective** (7.15 GiB read per token in bf16); 32 threads worse (91 ms, p90 146 ms). Scaled to MXFP4 bytes (1.75 GiB/token) at the same 94 GB/s: **≈20 ms/token → ~40 tok/s** with 5 ms of GPU work; at E1b's 125 GB/s read ceiling ≈15 ms → 50 tok/s. So on this CPU a bf16 GEMV already runs at 75 % of the DRAM read ceiling — compute is *not* the first bound; bandwidth is. Two variants in the script are **invalid as written** and their implied ceilings must be ignored: `read` used a bf16 `torch.sum` (10 GB/s; conversion-bound, not a DRAM probe — E1b replaces it with fp32/int64) and `int8-deq` (584 ms/token; torch temporaries, not a compute proxy — a real MXFP4 CPU kernel is KTransformers' or llama.cpp's). [`evidence/e6-stage0-cpu-expert-gemv.json`](../evidence/e6-stage0-cpu-expert-gemv.json).
- **Stage 1 — sglang-kt with V4-Flash on 2 GPUs (GPU).** Docker `approachingai/ktransformers:DSV4-specific`, `--kt-method MXFP4 --kt-num-gpu-experts N --kt-expert-placement-strategy frequency --kt-enable-dynamic-expert-update --kt-gpu-prefill-token-threshold 4096`, model V4-Flash (validated by KT). Sweep N from all-on-GPU down to 58% and record decode vs the resident 200 tok/s baseline. Purpose: prove the toolchain on SM120 (flashinfer ≥ 0.6.9, tilelang) and calibrate the calculator's `--gpu-ms-per-token` and `--dram-gbps` against a real hybrid before touching V4.1.
- **Stage 2 — port `deepseek_v41` into sglang-kt (build).** Design doc first (`plan/E6-DESIGN.md`): CSA2 attention and indexer, Engram host table (reuse SGLang `dev-dsv41`'s `SGLANG_ENABLE_DSV41_ENGRAM_HOST_TABLE`), hyper-connections, DSpark, and the MXFP4 expert format mapping (upstream UE8M0 g32 → KT's MXFP4 CPU kernel). Then serve V4.1-Flash with `--kt-num-gpu-experts` ≈ 8,958 total, Engram on NVMe + cache, and run `verify.py` + E8's benchmark set.
- **Parallel route — llama.cpp `deepseek41`.** Build vcruz305/llama.cpp `runtime/deepseek41`, load `vcruz305/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-GGUF` Q4_K_M (or Q3_K_M, 323 GiB) with `-ot` regex offload of ~42% of `ffn_*_exps` to CPU and `--split-mode layer`, once CSA2 is merged. Lower GPU efficiency than SGLang but zero porting.
- Pass: any route producing a V4.1-Flash decode/prefill/needle table on 2 GPUs. Record the engine commit, expert split, Engram mode and power in BENCHMARKS.md. Negative results (route blocked, number, reason) are recorded too.

## E7 — Routing skew study for expert pruning (GPU, uses the V4-Flash baseline deployment)

- Question: is routing uniform enough that pruning 42% of experts would destroy quality, or skewed enough that frequency-based GPU placement (E6) gains and a pruned checkpoint is plausible?
- Hypothesis: `noaux_tc` load balancing makes per-expert activation frequency within ±30% of uniform on general text; pruning is not viable without retraining.
- Procedure: enable expert-load logging on the V4-Flash baseline (same MoE family, 256 experts) over a 5 M-token mixed corpus; compute per-layer activation histograms and the cumulative share of the top-61% experts.
- Pass: report the share of activations captured by the top 58% of experts per layer; that number is `--activation-skew` for the calculator and the expected gain of frequency placement. If the top 58% capture ≥ 90% of activations on ≥ 80% of layers, Option D graduates to a build task; otherwise it is closed with the histogram as evidence.

## E8 — Baseline re-measurement (GPU, existing V4-Flash deployment)

- Question: what does the qualified V4-Flash-0731 NVFP4 TP2 deployment do under *this* harness?
- Hypothesis: within ±5% of the numbers in the sibling repo (200 tok/s single-stream with DSpark K5, ~226 tok/s at C2, ~2K tok/s prefill).
- Procedure: `scripts/bench-decode.py --concurrency 1,2,4,8 --runs 5 --warmup 2`, `scripts/bench-prefill.py --tokens 8192,32768,131072,524288,1000000`, `scripts/verify.py --needles 6 --context 1000000`.
- Pass: tables in BENCHMARKS.md; JSON in `evidence/baseline-v4-flash/`.
- **Result (run, 2026-09-21, host idle, `max_num_seqs=2`):** C1 decode **171–188 tok/s** median with the 256-token fixture (two 5-run sets, acceptance 40–45 %), **207–239 tok/s** with 512 tokens (acceptance 51–63 %) — the ±5 % hypothesis fails at 256 tokens and passes at 512; the variable is DSpark acceptance, which follows the generated content, not the GPUs. C2 354 tok/s summed (300 wall-clock). C4/C8 summed figures (659/1,239) are artefacts of the harness summing per-sequence rates while the server queues beyond `max_num_seqs=2` (TTFT 0.77/2.5 s); `bench-decode.py` now also prints `wall tok/s` (301/277). Prefill **6,123 / 7,810 / 6,157 tok/s** at 8K/32K/128K, **3,237** at 512K (1 run); the "~2K tok/s" in the hypothesis was the sibling's 1M-token number — prefill throughput is a length curve, not a scalar. `verify.py` at 222K tokens: 6/6 needles, TTFT 42.4 s. GPU draw: decode 242/249 W per card (uncapped), 32K prefill pinned at 300 W with SM clocks down to 1,950 MHz. Evidence: [`evidence/baseline-v4-flash/`](../evidence/baseline-v4-flash/) (`e8-decode.json`, `e8-prefill.json`, `e8-prefill-512k.json`, `e8-verify-262k.json`, `power-*.csv`). **DSpark-off single stream (relaunched with `DSPARK=0` via `RECIPE_ENV_FILE`, later the same day): 89.2 / 89.4 tok/s at 512 / 256 tokens, spread ±0.2 %, C2 161 summed; 216 / 225 W per card** — the 11.2 ms/step floor under every DSpark row. **1M prefill (run later the same day, DSpark on): 972,187 tokens in 512.2 s = 1,898 tok/s**, −2 % vs the sibling's 1,944; 300 W sustained for 489 s, SM 1.87 / 1.82 GHz, 88 / 87 °C peak. E8 is complete.
- **E8b result (run by owner with sudo, 2026-09-21; caps 325/300/275/250 — 325 W is `power.max_limit`, 250 W is `power.min_limit`, 225 W does not exist).** Decode: 229 / 216 / 226 / 226 tok/s — **cap-insensitive**; the cards draw 228–251 W uncapped at 2.26–2.31 GHz at every setting, the 300 W dip is DSpark acceptance (51 % vs 54–56 %), and the ±20 W draw spread is card temperature (44 °C at the first cap → 74 °C at the last). Prefill 32K: **8,770 / 8,428 / 7,991 / 7,482 tok/s**, each pinned at its cap to 0.5 W, SM 2.09 / 1.98 / 1.86 / 1.72 GHz; tok/s/W 13.6 / 14.0 / 14.5 / 15.0. So 250 W costs 0 % decode and 11 % prefill for 17 % less power (prediction was 0 / ~15 %); 325 W buys +4 % prefill for +8 % power. Decides T4.0 in favour of "250 W cap on the 1,500 W PSU" as the experimental option. Two harness bugs found and fixed: the first run's 275/250 W prefill rows were prefix-cache hits (same seeded prompt at every cap → now `777 + cap`; summarizer flags TTFT < 1 s), and a sampler leaked from a run that died on "connection refused" NUL-filled the next run's `power-decode-300w.csv` (sampler now killed from the EXIT trap; summarizer drops non-sample lines). Evidence: [`evidence/e8b-power-sweep/`](../evidence/e8b-power-sweep/).
- **E8b — power sweep (same window).** The host's PSU is 1,500 W; four Max-Q cards at 300 W do not fit it (HARDWARE.md power table). Repeat the C1 decode and 32K prefill rows at `nvidia-smi -pl 300`, `275`, `250`, `225` on both cards, logging `power.draw` and, if a wall meter is available, whole-system draw. Hypothesis: decode (bandwidth-bound) loses < 10 % from 300 → 225 W; prefill (compute-bound) loses 15–25 %. Output: the tok/s-per-watt curve that decides between "cap four cards at 225 W on the 1,500 W PSU" and "buy a 2,000 W PSU" for Option A. Restore `-pl 300` afterwards.

## E9 — 4-GPU V4.1-Flash qualification (blocked on hardware)

- Question: does the SGLang `dev-dsv41` + Engram-in-DRAM profile serve V4.1-Flash on 4 × RTX PRO 6000 Max-Q, and how does it compare with 0xSero's NVMe-Engram numbers and with E8?
- Hypothesis: 180–230 tok/s at C1 with DSpark 5, 600–750 tok/s at C8, 5–7K tok/s prefill, DSpark acceptance 75–88%; Engram-in-DRAM improves prefill TTFT on long prompts versus NVMe mode by a measurable margin.
- Procedure: `preflight.sh` (must pass on 4 GPUs), `serve.sh --profile 4gpu-sglang`, `wait-ready.py`, `verify.py`, E8's benchmark set, then the same with `OFFLOAD_MODE=nvme` for the A/B.
- Pass: verify 6/6 needles at 262,144 and at the configured maximum; `17*19=323` sanity; tables recorded with power condition (300 W Max-Q).

## E10 — Speculative decoding under a CPU-bound MoE (GPU, after E6 stage 1)

- Status: **superseded by E23** (2026-09-22) — same question, but as a multi-round tuning program with the counters E21 taught us to read; the traffic model below stays as the prior.
- Question: does DSpark (block 5) help or hurt when 42% of expert activations are DRAM-bound?
- Hypothesis: a 5-token block activates ~3–4× the distinct CPU experts of a single token, so DRAM traffic per step rises faster than accepted tokens per step; net decode falls unless acceptance ≥ 80%. Community reports on CPU-bandwidth-bound hybrids say spec decode hurts.
- Procedure: on the E6 stage 1 (V4-Flash) and stage 2 (V4.1-Flash) deployments, C1 decode with DSpark off, block 3, block 5; log accepted tokens/step and host DRAM bandwidth (`perf stat` uncore counters or `pcm-memory`).
- Pass: tok/s and GiB/step per setting; the recipe's default follows the measurement.

## E11 — Engram tiering and quantization (GPU-free data prep; GPU for quality)

- Question: which Engram placement is best for the 2-GPU hybrid, where DRAM is the scarce tier?
- Hypothesis: NVMe + 64 GiB DRAM row cache costs < 5% decode versus DRAM-resident tables (48 reads/token at ~40 µs each, parallel) and adds ~8 s per 64K-token prefill; FP4 or q3_K tables in DRAM remove that cost at an unmeasured quality price.
- Procedure: (a) extend `bench-engram-gather.py` with an LRU/hot-set cache in front of NVMe and a Zipf request distribution to estimate hit rate vs cache size; (b) once E6 serves, A/B NVMe-cache vs LibertAI FP4 tables on `verify.py` needles and a small perplexity set.
- Pass: decode and prefill delta per mode; a quality verdict on quantized tables.
- Result (2026-10-11, T3.10 → **E40**): (a) done GPU-free with the serve's own hash map instead of Zipf (`scripts/engram-working-set.py`): real text touches ~1 row per 4 KiB page, 190 KiB of page cache per first-touched token, 75.6 GiB over the 262K × 4 smoke; LRU 38 % at 16 GiB, 55 % at 86 GiB. (b) **not run, rejected with evidence**: re-quantized tables cannot shrink the page-cache footprint (one row per page either way), and the measured Engram problem was the page cache being pinned by stale weight-shard pages, fixed in `wait-ready.py`. The hypothesis' "64 GiB DRAM row cache" survives as T3.48 (user-space 264 B rows, ~22× denser than page cache).

## E12 — Kernel census on SM120 (GPU, uses the V4-Flash baseline deployment first)

- Question: which kernels do SGLang `dev-dsv41`, vLLM and sglang-kt actually run on these cards for the MoE experts, attention and DSpark, and how many ms/token do they cost? The calculator's `--gpu-ms-per-token 5` is an assumption taken from the V4-Flash TP2 result; this replaces it with a breakdown.
- Hypothesis: the routed-expert GEMMs run on Marlin W4A16 (FP4 dequantised to FP16, no FP4 tensor-core use) rather than a native SM120 block-scaled path; experts are ≥ 50% of GPU time at batch 1; attention (CSA2/MLA via FlashInfer) ≤ 25%.
- Procedure: `nsys profile --trace cuda,nvtx` of 200 decode steps and one 32K prefill on the V4-Flash baseline, then on each V4.1 engine as it becomes runnable; server logs grepped for backend selection (`Marlin`, `cutlass`, `flashinfer`, `trtllm`, `triton`). Record top-10 kernels by time, ms/token per category, and whether any kernel is a documented SM120 fallback.
- Pass: a table in BENCHMARKS.md; the calculator's `--gpu-ms-per-token` becomes a measured value with a breakdown. Also records DSpark acceptance under the dispatched path (feeds E10: acceptance drops are reported on the Marlin dequant path because MTP heads were trained on native FP4 activations).
- **Result (V4-Flash baseline, from the sibling's torch-profiler traces via `scripts/kernel-census.py`; V4.1 engines pending E6):** **Hypothesis refuted.** vLLM runs the NVFP4 routed experts on a **CUTLASS SM120 block-scaled PtrArray grouped GEMM** (`MainloopSm120ArrayTmaWarpSpecializedBlockScaled`, cooperative, 128×64 tile, `float_e2m1_t × float_ue4m3_t`) — E13's kernel family with NVFP4 types. Marlin is 0.24 ms/step (one small projection). Experts are **24.5 %** of summed kernel time, not ≥ 50 %; dense FP8 (`_w8a8_triton_block_scaled_mm`, `fp8_einsum`, `per_token_group_quant`) is the largest category at **27 %**, bf16 GEMMs 12 %, attention/indexer 11 % (15 % at 48K context), DSpark draft 10 %, hyper-connections 4 %, all-reduce 3 % (16 % at C2). Union GPU time **15.3 ms per 6-token verify step** → 3.8–5.1 ms per accepted token at the measured 40–63 % acceptance; `--gpu-ms-per-token 5` stays as the conservative value. `nsys` could not be used (cannot attach to the running container); the 32K prefill census is still open and will use vLLM's `--profiler-config` route. Evidence: [`evidence/e12-kernel-census-v4-flash-tp2.json`](../evidence/e12-kernel-census-v4-flash-tp2.json); table in BENCHMARKS.md.

## E13 — Custom MXFP4 grouped GEMM for SM120 (build GPU-free; measure in a GPU window)

- Question: how much of the Marlin penalty can a native SM120 block-scaled kernel recover for V4.1-Flash's expert shapes (per expert: gate/up [4608 × 5120], down [5120 × 2304], MXFP4 E2M1 with UE8M0 block-32 scales; decode batch 1–8 tokens × 6 experts; prefill 4096-token chunks)?
- Hypothesis: CUTLASS's SM120 block-scaled grouped GEMM (SM120 mainloops since CUTLASS 3.9; mixed-input block-scaled grouped GEMM since 4.2; MoE grouped GEMM API in 4.3), tiled for 99 KiB shared memory (no TMEM/tcgen05 on SM120), reaches ≥ 2× Marlin W4A16 throughput at prefill shapes and ≥ 1.3× at decode shapes, where the GEMV is bandwidth-bound and dequant cost is what Marlin pays.
- Format question (answered from source, RESEARCH.md "MXFP4 is native"): SM120 accepts MXFP4 E2M1 + UE8M0 block-32 directly via `mma.sync … kind::mxf4nvf4 … scale_vec::2X … ue8m0` on `sm_120a`; CUTLASS's SM120 block-scaled builder takes `cutlass::mx_float4_t<float_e2m1_t>` (SF vector 32) and ships a dense `sm120_bs_gemm_mxf4_mxf4` unit test. No re-quantisation to NVFP4/UE4M3 is needed; the community claim came from CUTLASS 3.8. What is still untested anywhere is the mxf4 × mxf4 **grouped** (PtrArray) instantiation on SM120 — that is stage 1.
- Procedure, stage 1 (build GPU-free, run in a GPU window):
  1. Start from `examples/79_blackwell_geforce_gemm/79d_blackwell_geforce_nvfp4_grouped_gemm.cu` (CUTLASS ≥ 4.2; `ArchTag Sm120`, `OpClassBlockScaledTensorOp`, TMA warp-specialized cooperative and pingpong, tile 128×128×128). Swap `ElementA/ElementB` from `cutlass::nv_float4_t<float_e2m1_t>` to `cutlass::mx_float4_t<float_e2m1_t>` (builder derives `float_ue8m0_t`, SF vector 32 → `BlockScaledInstr::MXF4_NVF4`); replace the FP4-output `LinCombBlockScaleFactor` epilogue with a plain bf16 `LinearCombination` so D is bf16 like the MoE down-projection expects. Build with `-gencode arch=compute_120a,code=sm_120a` (the `kind::mxf4*` PTX is arch-specific; `compute_120f` is not enough) in `dsv4-sm120:cuda-devel-https` (CUDA ≥ 12.8 required for the 2X-UE8M0 atom; check the image's `nvcc --version`).
  2. Second variant for W4A8: A as `cutlass::mx_float8_t<float_e4m3_t>`, B as `mx_float4_t` (the `kind::mxf8f6f4` mixed path example 79c uses). Same build.
  3. Feed real V4.1 expert shapes: per expert gate/up [4608 × 5120] and down [5120 × 2304] as K-major B; grouped problem lists for decode (6 experts × 1–8 tokens, M padded to the tile) and prefill (4096-token chunks spread over 384 experts ≈ 64 tokens/expert). Random E2M1 payload and UE8M0 scales in the checkpoint's block-32 layout.
  4. Correctness: compare against a host/torch reference that dequantises B (and A, in the same block-32 scheme) to fp32 and multiplies; W4A4 and W4A8 must match their own dequant reference bit-exactly in fp32 accumulate order tolerance (≤ 1 ulp bf16 after rounding). Also record W4A4 and W4A8 error against a **bf16-activation** reference so the activation-quantisation cost is visible, since Marlin W4A16 pays none.
  5. Speed: TFLOP/s and effective weight GB/s for each variant versus vLLM's Marlin MoE kernel on the same shapes (`VLLM_MOE_FORCE_MARLIN=1` path, run inside `dsv4-nvfp4:recipe`).
- Stage 2, if stage 1 wins: wire the kernel into the engine chosen by E6 (sglang-kt or SGLang `dev-dsv41`) behind the existing MoE backend selection, and re-run E12.
- Pass: microbench numbers recorded either way in `results/e13-stage1.json`. Stage 2 proceeds only if stage 1 shows ≥ 1.3× over Marlin at decode shapes with the variant's activation-quantisation error judged acceptable (W4A8 preferred if W4A4 error is visible in `verify.py`).
- **Stage 1 result (built and run; source `kernels/e13/`):** the mxf4 × mxf4 grouped PtrArray GEMM **compiles and runs on SM120** from CUTLASS v4.7.1 with a plain type swap (`nv_float4_t` → `mx_float4_t`, BF16 `LinearCombination` epilogue), built with `-gencode arch=compute_120a,code=sm_120a` in `dsv4-sm120:cuda-devel-https` (nvcc 13.0) in 34 s; every decode case **passes CUTLASS's host block-scaled reference check**, both cooperative and pingpong schedules. Numbers at V4.1 expert shapes (6 groups for decode, 384 × M=64 for prefill; `gemm.run()` only, 200 iterations; GPU 0 at 300 W):

  | Case | M/group | Groups | pingpong µs | TFLOPS | Weight GB/s |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
  | gate/up N=4608 K=5120 | 1 | 6 | 53.8 | 5.3 | **1,397** |
  | gate/up | 4 | 6 | 62.4 | 18.1 | 1,205 |
  | gate/up | 8 | 6 | 54.7 | 41.4 | 1,374 |
  | down N=5120 K=2304 | 1 | 6 | 39.6 | 3.6 | 949 |
  | down | 4 | 6 | 34.4 | 16.5 | 1,094 |
  | down | 8 | 6 | 32.1 | 35.3 | 1,171 |
  | gate/up prefill | 64 | 384 | 3,462 (coop) | **335** | 1,390 |
  | down prefill | 64 | 384 | 1,939 (coop) | 299 | 1,241 |
  | gate/up dense proxy | 4,096 | 6 | 1,094 | **1,060** | — |
  | down dense proxy | 4,096 | 6 | 624 | 930 | — |

  Reading: decode is **weight-bandwidth bound at 93 % of the measured GDDR7 copy rate** (1,397 of ~1,470 GB/s) for gate/up; the down projection's shorter K (18 mainloop iterations, 240 CTAs) leaves it at 65–80 %. Per layer, one token's 6 experts cost ≈ 54 + 40 = **94 µs → 3.8 ms for all 40 layers** on one GPU at M=1 (before the TP2 split halves the bytes per card). Prefill at 64 tokens/expert is 300–335 TFLOPS; a dense 4,096-row proxy reaches 1,060 TFLOPS, about 53 % of the 2 PFLOPS dense FP4 spec at the Max-Q 300 W cap. K3 tile sweep (128×64×128 and 128×64×256, same run): **worse at every decode M** (gate/up M=1 870–950 GB/s; down 590–650) — more, shorter CTAs cost more than the extra parallelism buys when the 128-row M tile is 99 % padding. K4: pingpong beats cooperative by 1–5 % at decode, cooperative by 6–8 % at 384-group prefill. Re-running `gemm.initialize()` inside the timed loop (the example's default) adds only ~1 µs. **K2 W4A8 (`E13_A8=1`, A = MXFP8 E4M3, `kind::mxf8f6f4`) built and verified in the same window but is ~2× slower at decode**: gate/up M=1 103.8 µs (724 GB/s), down 58.6 µs (642 GB/s) — [`e13-stage1-decode-w4a8.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-decode-w4a8.json). The reason is instructive: the 128-row M tile means an M=1 group issues the MMA work of M=128 (6 groups × 2·128·4608·5120 = 36 GFLOP ≈ 36 µs at the ~1 PFLOPS the dense proxy showed), so **W4A4 at 54 µs is already two-thirds MMA-issue time, not bandwidth**, and the FP8-rate `mxf8f6f4` MMA (half the FP4 rate) pushes W4A8 over the edge into compute-bound. A smaller M tile is therefore the decode lever for both variants — but `TILE_M=64` fails to compile: the SM120 TMA warp-specialized mainloop static-asserts a 128-row CTA tile (`copy_traits_sm90_tma.hpp:745`). A decode-shaped kernel (M tile 16–32, cp.async or plain-LDG mainloop feeding `mma.sync kind::mxf4nvf4` directly) is a new kernel, not a template knob: **K10**. Still open: K5 Marlin denominator (`VLLM_MOE_FORCE_MARLIN=1` in `dsv4-nvfp4:recipe`), the bf16-activation error comparison (step 4), K10. Evidence: [`e13-stage1-mxf4-grouped-gemm.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-mxf4-grouped-gemm.json) (with per-iteration initialize), [`e13-stage1-decode-noreinit.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-decode-noreinit.json), tile variants [`…-n64k128.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-decode-tile-n64k128.json), [`…-n64k256.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-decode-tile-n64k256.json).
- **Stage 1 addendum (2026-09-21, second GPU window — census-shaped runs):** `run.py --tp 2 --decode-groups 36` reproduces the deployed engine's per-rank shapes (gate/up N=2,304 K=5,120; down N=5,120 K=1,152) and a 6-token DSpark verify step's worst-case expert count. Results (pingpong, `--no-reinit`, 200 iterations): gate/up M=1/4/8 **168–172 µs = 1,314–1,343 GB/s**; down **79–87 µs = 1,301–1,435 GB/s**. TP1 at 36 groups (451 MB of weights, 3.5× the 128 MB L2): gate/up **311–318 µs = 1,421–1,451 GB/s**, down 165–170 µs = 1,327–1,369 GB/s. So the original 6-group figures were not L2-assisted; the kernel sits at 97–99 % of the E2 copy ceiling once the working set exceeds L2. At 15 groups (94 MB, fits L2) gate/up M=1 is 72.5 µs — the same as the deployed CUTLASS NVFP4 kernel's 73 µs launch in the E12 census — but the M=4 row's 1,611 GB/s is above HBM and must be read as L2 hits. **Conclusion for E13 step 5:** the Marlin denominator (K5) is moot for the vLLM NVFP4 path, which already runs a roofline CUTLASS SM120 grouped GEMM; E13's advantage is format (MXFP4/UE8M0 as V4.1 ships) and integration into an engine that lacks an SM120 MXFP4 path, not speed. Evidence: [`evidence/e13-stage1-decode-tp2-g36.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-decode-tp2-g36.json), [`e13-stage1-decode-tp2-g15.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-decode-tp2-g15.json), [`e13-stage1-decode-tp1-g36.json`](../evidence/e13-stage1-decode-tp1-g36.json).
- Known prior art to read first: FlashInfer #2577 and PR #2725 (SM120 NVFP4 MoE patches), CUTLASS #3096 (TMA WS grouped GEMM failing on SM120; `compute_120f` fix → 39 tok/s native FP4), TRT-LLM #12706 (`TRTLLM_MOE_BACKEND=TRITON` 6.7× over CUTLASS on SM120/121), Colfax "NVFP4 blockscaled GEMM on SM12x", chsasank/blackwell custom CUTLASS NVFP4 kernels (GEMM at 38% of peak).

## E15 — What CUDA graphs are worth on this box (GPU, one V4-Flash relaunch; informs the E6 engine design)

- Question: both incumbents decode under CUDA graphs (GLM `--max-cudagraph-capture-size 96`, V4-Flash `MAX_CUDAGRAPH_CAPTURE_SIZE=16`). The V4.1 paths may not be able to: SGLang `dev-dsv41` reports capture crashes on SM120 (vLLM #56700 lineage), and sglang-kt's hybrid step calls into CPU experts mid-graph. How much decode do we lose in eager mode, and does the loss scale with concurrency and with speculation (a K5 speculative step launches ~6× the kernels)?
- Hypothesis: the E12 census shows several hundred launches per decode step (236 `_w8a8_triton_block_scaled_mm` alone) inside an 11–15 ms step; at ~5–10 µs CPU launch cost each plus Python scheduler time, eager costs 15–35 % at C1 with DSpark on, less without DSpark (fewer launches, longer step), and less again at C8+ where the GPU work per step grows and hides the CPU.
- Procedure: relaunch the sibling with `ENFORCE_EAGER=1` (or the equivalent vLLM flag via `RECIPE_ENV_FILE`) and re-run `bench-decode.py --concurrency 1,2,8 --max-tokens 512` with DSpark on and off; compare to E8 rows. Record per-card power and SM util — eager shows as util gaps between kernels. Optional: `nsys`/torch-profiler one step to count launches and gaps directly.
- Pass: a table eager vs graphs at C1/C2/C8 × DSpark on/off; a number for "cost of eager" that the E6 design uses to decide whether the CPU-expert call must be graph-compatible (`cudaLaunchHostFunc` / KTransformers' cpuinfer submit-and-sync pattern) or whether eager is acceptable for a 2-GPU hybrid whose step is already bandwidth-bound on DRAM.
- **Result (2026-09-21, three relaunches at 262K / 16 slots):** graphs+DSpark C1 **217** → eager+DSpark **49.5** (4.4×) → eager no-DSpark **15.9 tok/s** (63 ms/step vs 11.2 graphed, 5.6×); at C16 1,258 → 618 → 249. Eager GPUs draw 142–155 W at 2.3 GHz (starved). Hypothesis undershot badly: the cost is ≈52 ms/step of Python dispatch + lost torch.compile fusion, not 5 ms of launch gaps. **Decision for E6:** the GPU step must be captured/compiled; the CPU-expert call must be graph-compatible. **T2.4 close-out (same window):** util 0.9651 makes 1M + 16 slots start (KV 1,184,027 tokens, 1.18× a 1M request); C1 209 / C4 535 / C16 1,086 wall; 1M prefill 972,187 tok in 504 s — nothing lost vs the shipped profile. DSpark draft forward graph (`dspark_forward_cudagraph` + `_allow_tp`): captured shape `(5,)` only, C1 step 18.0 vs 17.8 ms — neutral, rejected. Bonus: speculation amortises a CPU-bound step 3× (E10 preview), acceptance unchanged in eager mode. Evidence [`evidence/e15-t24-v4-flash/`](../evidence/e15-t24-v4-flash/); table in BENCHMARKS.md §E15.

## E16 — GLM-5.3-Flash at 32 slots: is the reference recipe leaving throughput on the table? (GPU, one GLM relaunch)

- Question: E14 saw GLM's C-scaling still rising at 16 slots (424 → 569 wall tok/s) and vLLM reporting a 2.96M-token KV pool (2.7 × a 1M request). BENCHMARKS §GLM listed 32 slots + capture 192 as the top untested tune. Does it pay, and is the KV pool really that free?
- Hypothesis: C32 > 800 tok/s wall if the EXL3 dequant leaves SM headroom; smaller gain than V4-Flash's 3.6× because GLM is compute-pinned at 300 W from C2.
- Procedure: GLM `serve.sh` hard-coded `--max-cudagraph-capture-size 96`; changed it to derive `MAX_NUM_SEQS × (DFLASH_TOKENS + 1)` and raised the validator cap to 32 (committed in the GLM repo, `324f478`). Relaunch with `ENV_FILE=/tmp/glm-seqs32.env` (only `MAX_NUM_SEQS=32` differs), `bench-decode.py --concurrency 1,16,32 --runs 5 --warmup 2 --max-tokens 512`, watch `Running/Waiting/KV usage` in the engine log and per-card power/temp.
- Pass: C32 wall tok/s vs C16, TTFT, and whether the scheduler actually runs 32.
- **Result (2026-09-21):** engine reported KV 16.19 GiB = 2,833,485 tokens, 27 capture sizes up to 192 (73 s, 0.30 GiB). But **each request pins ≈ 4.9 % of the pool regardless of length** (1 req 4.6–5.3 %, 3 → 15.8 %, 16 → 74–83 %, 20 → 95–99 %): the Mamba per-sequence state under `--mamba-cache-mode align` (~0.8 GiB per slot), so **≈ 20 sequences is the real ceiling**. At C32 the scheduler ran 19–21 with 9–13 waiting. C1 133.3 (41.6 % accept) / C16 **571.9** wall (TTFT 0.61 s) / C32 **578.9** wall (TTFT 0.88 s): +1 % throughput, +44 % latency. Cards pinned at 300–304 W, **86–87 °C**, 1.82–1.85 GHz. **Decision: keep 16 slots**; the derived-capture-size script change stays (identical at 16). Corrects E14: the "2.7 × 1M headroom" figure is attention-only; a 1M GLM request also needs its Mamba slot, and 20 slots × 4.9 % is the binding constraint for short requests. Evidence [`evidence/e16-glm-32slots/`](../evidence/e16-glm-32slots/).

## E17 — One MoE layer through kt-kernel on the host: does the CPU expert tier fit and how fast is it? (GPU-free apart from the reference; T3.6b)

- Question: E6-DESIGN §4.1 assumed the CPU tier costs 17.9 MiB per expert (checkpoint bytes) and 112.8 GiB total, and that upstream's `KTEPWrapperMethod` + `kt-kernel 0.7.0.post4` would run V4.1-Flash's MXFP4 experts unchanged. Before a 40-layer serve, load **one** MoE layer the way the engine will (`MXFP4SafeTensorLoader` → `KTMoEWrapper`), measure host RSS, check the output against an independent dequant reference, and time the CPU path at decode- and prefill-shaped token counts.
- Hypothesis: 2.8 GiB anonymous memory per layer (161 CPU experts × 17.9 MiB), rel-L2 error ≤ 1e-2 vs an FP32 dequant reference, T=1 latency ≈ 1 ms/layer (131 MiB of expert weights at the 125 GB/s E1b ceiling).
- Procedure: `scripts/test-kt-one-layer.py` inside `dsv41-kt:recipe` with the overlay bind-mounted (command in the script header): load layer 5 with `--num-gpu-experts 224`, snapshot anon RSS before/after, forward `T=8` random tokens (half with one slot routed to a GPU expert id), compare to a reference that dequantises E2M1 × 2^(UE8M0−127) on the GPU and applies the same SwiGLU-with-clamp; sweep `--threads 8/16/32`, `--threadpool-count 2`, `--x-scale 8` (drives activations past the 10.0 clamp), `--swiglu-limit 0`, and `--bench-cold` (fresh ids each iteration).
- Pass: per-layer RSS that extrapolates to ≤ 150 GiB over 40 layers, rel-L2 ≤ 1e-2 including the clamp, T=1 ≤ 1.5 ms.
- **Result (2026-09-21):** (1) **Memory model surprise:** kt-kernel's C++ `load_weights` repacks *all 384* experts into anonymous host memory regardless of `gpu_experts_mask` — 8.0 GiB/layer flat, 330 GiB for the model. KTransformers assumes the CPU holds every expert and the GPU holds hot copies. Fixed in the overlay with a **compact CPU view** (loader returns only the 160 CPU experts renumbered 0..159; router ids remapped in `submit`, GPU slots → −1): **3.4 GiB/layer = 21.8 MiB per CPU expert** (+22 % over checkpoint bytes: UE8M0→BF16 scales plus AVX-512 tile layout) → **136 GiB** for 40 layers at 224/160. Load 0.9 s/layer. (2) **Correctness:** rel-L2 **3.8e-3** (max abs 0.021 at |y|max 4.4) — bf16-level; GPU-slot tokens match the GPU-only reference exactly. `swiglu_limit` **must be passed** (×8 inputs: kt matches the clamped reference at 2.8e-3, is 0.77 off the unclamped one; `--swiglu-limit 0` flips it). Two upstream bugs found and fixed on the way: the routed scaling factor 1.5 was applied twice (`fuse_routed_scaling_factor_in_topk` leaked through `__getattr__`), and the wrapper was built with the 0.6 API (`gpu_experts_mask` is positional, `swiglu_limit` missing). (3) **Timing, 16 threads, cold ids:** T=1 **1.08 ms** = 127 GB/s — the E1b fabric ceiling to within 2 %, so the tier is DRAM-bound at T=1; T=4 4.0, T=8 7.6, T=16 13.8, T=64 39.8 ms (per-token cost flat until T≈16, amortises past 64); warm T=256 65 ms. **8 threads 2× slower, 32 threads (SMT) 7× slower, threadpool 2 no change** — 16 cores × ~8 GB/s each is exactly the fabric. Warm ids over-report (160 GB/s) because the 64 MiB L3 holds half of a T=1 working set. Floor: 2.5 CPU experts/token/layer × 21.8 MiB × 40 = 2.2 GB → 18 ms → **≈ 55 tok/s single-stream** if fully overlapped (calculator: 50; B2 split 77). Evidence [`evidence/t36b-kt-one-layer/`](../evidence/t36b-kt-one-layer/) (`layer5-compact-gpu224.json` correctness, `layer5-*-cold.json` timing, `layer5-clamp-xscale8.json` / `layer5-noclamp-xscale8.json` clamp, `mem-gpu304.json` and `layer5-patched.json` the un-compacted 8 GiB rows). Overlay commit `3805730e` on `dsv41-kt-hybrid`.
- Open: `frequency` placement needs the loader to take an explicit id list (compact view is a prefix split today); mlock/hugepages for the 136 GiB (T3.6e); whether the B2 split can also drive PCIe reads of DRAM experts from the GPU side.

## E18 — Engram `file` layout (A-min): what does a row lookup cost when the 94 GiB table stays in the checkpoint shard? (GPU-free; T3.6c)

- Question: E6-DESIGN §4.3 chose A-min — leave both Engram tables (188.8 GiB) on NVMe and let the page cache be the row cache — on the strength of E3's synthetic gather (1.9 ms per 48 rows in Python). Before the first serve, measure the real thing: the two `layers.{1,14}.engram.embed.{weight,scale}` tensors in the V4.1-Flash snapshot, cold and warm, at decode (T=1) and chunked-prefill (T=4096, 196,608 rows) shapes, and check that the rows read from the file are bit-exact against `safetensors`.
- Hypothesis: one contiguous tensor per table in one shard (so `mmap` of the shard + row offset is the whole layout); cold T=1 ≈ 2 ms via serial page faults, ≈ 1 ms if the 48 row faults are issued in parallel; a 4096-token chunk ≈ 1 s cold (E3's 5 µs/row) and ≈ 5 ms warm.
- Procedure: `scripts/bench-engram-file.py --snapshot /hfm/snapshots/<hash> --json /out/layer1-cold.json` inside `dsv41-kt:recipe` (no `--gpus`, no overlay needed); page cache dropped first (`echo 3 > /proc/sys/vm/drop_caches`, owner runs). Five strategies per shape: `torch` (`index_select` on the `frombuffer` tensor, serial faults), `preadv-16/64` (thread pool doing `os.preadv` per row), `willneed-16/64` (thread pool issuing `madvise(MADV_WILLNEED)` per row then one `index_select`). Cold = fresh random ids each run; warm = repeat the same ids.
- Pass: bit-exact rows; a strategy with cold T=1 ≤ 1.5 ms and 4096-chunk ≤ 1 s — that is, the Engram tier costs less than one MoE layer on the CPU tier (E17: 1.08 ms) at decode and less than the 1.06 s expert transfer per chunk at prefill.
- **Result (2026-09-21, cold page cache after reboot):** (1) **Layout:** each table is one contiguous tensor in one shard — `layers.1.engram.embed.weight` F8_E4M3 `[384,006,168, 256]` + `.scale` F8_E8M0 `[384,006,168, 8]` in `model-00047-of-00048.safetensors`, layer 14 in `model-00048`; 94.4 GiB per table, **264 B per row** (256 weight + 8 scale, in two separate byte ranges of the same file). A-min is ~150 lines, not 40, because the two ranges must be resolved from the shard header and the `EngramEmbedding` fast path assumed a `cudaHostRegister`ed table. (2) **Cold NVMe, 16 threads, T=1 (48 rows):** `torch` **6.1 ms** (serial faults, 128 µs/row = one NVMe round trip each), `preadv-16` 1.67 ms, **`willneed-16` 1.02 ms** (21 µs/row) — hypothesis confirmed, and WILLNEED beats preadv because the kernel issues the 96 reads without a Python thread holding each. 64 threads are slower than 16 at every shape (1.97 vs 1.02 ms) — thread spawn overhead exceeds the I/O. (3) **T=16 (768 rows):** torch 58 ms, willneed-16 **5.0 ms**. (4) **T=4096 chunk (196,608 rows):** torch 1.41 s, preadv-16 1.17 s, **willneed-16 0.50 s = 2.5 µs/row** — 2× under the E3 estimate and half the 1.06 s expert transfer per chunk, so at prefill the Engram tier hides behind the expert tier if overlapped. (5) **Warm (page-cached):** torch 0.003 / 0.056 / 2.9 ms at T=1/16/4096 — the warm path is the plain gather; the willneed prefetch adds 0.7 ms of fixed thread cost at T=1 (0.72 ms) and 185 ms at T=4096, so the implementation should skip the prefetch when `mincore` says the rows are resident (open item). (6) **Bit-exact** for 64 random rows vs `safetensors` (`bit_exact_64_rows: true`). Evidence [`evidence/e18-engram-file/layer1-cold.json`](../evidence/e18-engram-file/layer1-cold.json).
- **Implementation (overlay `a1ca246a` on `dsv41-kt-hybrid`):** `engram.py` gains `_FileTable` (mmap of the shard, weight/scale offsets from the header, `_engram_file_rows` = WILLNEED prefetch on `SGLANG_DSV41_ENGRAM_FILE_THREADS` threads then `index_select` → dequant → H2D), wrapped as `bcg_engram_file_rows = eager_on_graph(True)(…)` so it is a graph break like the hasher already is; `EngramEmbedding` branches on layout `file`; `_load_rows` verifies shard edges instead of copying 94 GiB. Env: `SGLANG_ENABLE_DSV41_ENGRAM_HOST_TABLE=1 SGLANG_DSV41_ENGRAM_HOST_TABLE_LAYOUT=file SGLANG_DSV41_ENGRAM_FILE_DIR=<snapshot> SGLANG_DSV41_ENGRAM_FILE_THREADS=16`. The lookup raises if it is reached inside a live full-graph capture, so decode must run `--cuda-graph-backend-decode breakable`. Standalone TP2-shaped test (two rank halves summed = full lookup, bit-exact; warm 2.5–3.3 ms per rank) passed. `drop_checkpoint_page_cache` is only called from `_HostTable.finish_load`, so the file layout keeps the page cache it warms.
- Open: `mincore`-gated prefetch (skip the 0.7 ms thread cost on warm rows); issue layer-14 rows at layer 1 (A-full, §4.3) — with 1.0 ms cold at T=1 this is ~2 ms/token, 4 % of an 55-tok/s step, so A-full is not first-serve critical; hit rate under real text is still E11's question. Cost of the second graph break per Engram layer is a T3.6d measurement.

## E14 — Model-to-model on identical hardware: GLM-5.3-Flash vs V4-Flash vs V4.1-Flash (GPU, one window per model)

- Question: for *this* box — two RTX PRO 6000 Max-Q, 256 GiB DDR5-6400, 1,500 W PSU — which model is the better daily driver, measured with one harness, one fixture set and one power policy? The GLM recipe (`~/dev/src/glm-5.3-flash-2x-rtx-pro-6000-blackwell`, EXL3 K3 3.5 bpw + DFlash2 K5 draft, tpurtell runtime, `glm-5.3-flash-local` on :8000, `max_num_seqs` 16, 1,048,576 ctx) is the owner's known-good reference; its published numbers (C1 179.5 tok/s at 65 % DFlash acceptance, 6/6 needles at 1M in 285 s) were produced by its own harness and are not directly comparable to the E8 numbers here.
- Hypothesis: V4-Flash NVFP4 wins single-stream decode (≈ 228 vs ≈ 180 tok/s) on DSpark's higher accepted-tokens-per-step; GLM wins aggregate throughput at C8–C16 because its scheduler has 16 slots against V4-Flash's 2, and wins 1M-token prefill (285 s for 1M with retrieval vs V4-Flash's 514 s). Without speculation both sit near 90 tok/s single-stream (bandwidth floor of ~150 GiB of resident weights across two cards). tok/s/W at 250 W differs by < 10 %. V4.1-Flash column stays empty until E6 s2 lands.
- Procedure (per model, one GPU window each; both containers bind :8000 and want both cards, so never both at once):
  1. `bench-decode.py --concurrency 1,2,4,8,16 --runs 5 --warmup 2 --max-tokens 512` — record `wall tok/s` and acceptance; for GLM also read the DFlash acceptance the runtime reports.
  2. Same with speculation off (GLM: launch without the draft; V4-Flash: `DSPARK=0` via `RECIPE_ENV_FILE`).
  3. `bench-prefill.py --tokens 32768,131072,524288` (3 runs, fresh seed per model) and one 1M run.
  4. `verify.py --needles 6 --context 262144` and at 1M.
  5. `sudo bash scripts/bench-power-sweep.sh --caps 300,250` (owner runs; decode + prefill).
  6. Footprint: VRAM per card at idle after load, host RSS, disk, and the context ceiling / KV pool the engine reports.
- Pass: one table in BENCHMARKS.md ("E14 — same box, three models") with rows decode C1 (spec on/off), decode C8 wall, prefill 32K/128K/512K/1M, needles 262K/1M, tok/s/W at 300 and 250 W, VRAM/DRAM/disk footprint, max context, licence/commercial-use note (the DFlash2 draft is CC BY-NC-ND). JSON under `evidence/e14-<model>/`. The V4.1 column is filled by the same procedure the day E6 s2 serves.
- **Result, GLM column (run 2026-09-21, one GPU window):** C1 **132.5 tok/s** at 40 % DFlash acceptance (135 at 256 tok) vs V4-Flash 207–239 — hypothesis (a) confirmed; C16 wall-clock **569 tok/s** vs V4-Flash's 300 ceiling — (b) confirmed, and the 16-vs-2 slot difference is the reason; prefill **flat** 4,213 / 4,184 / 4,005 tok/s at 38K / 153K / 610K actual tokens and **3,829 tok/s at 1,001,032 tokens (261 s)** vs V4-Flash 1,898 (512 s) — (c) confirmed, crossover ≈ 400K; 6/6 needles at 251K, TTFT 59.7 s; VRAM 92.4 GiB/card, KV pool 2.96M tokens. **Unexpected:** GLM decode is hotter per token — 256–259 W per card at C1 (V4-Flash 233–250) and **pinned at 300 W for any concurrency C2→C16** (580-sample median 300.0 W, 1.9 GHz) — because EXL3 trellis dequant runs on the SMs before each GEMM. (First write-up said "297 W at C1"; that was the all-concurrency median — corrected after the sweep.) **250 W sweep (owner ran):** C1 decode 119.0 → 126.9 tok/s (acceptance 34 → 40 %, clocks 2.22 → 2.09 GHz, pinned at 250 W) — cap cost hidden inside acceptance noise; 32K prefill 4,462 → 3,943 tok/s (−11.6 %, same as V4-Flash's −11 %); tok/s/W 0.23 → 0.25 decode, 7.5 → 7.9 prefill. T4.0 holds for GLM. Not run: speculation-off C1 (recipe hard-requires the draft), footprint capture for V4-Flash. Harness note: `verify.py`'s thinking-toggle probe fails on GLM because it sends V4's template kwarg. Evidence [`evidence/e14-glm53/`](../evidence/e14-glm53/); table in BENCHMARKS.md §E14.
- **V4.1 target derived from E14:** to displace either incumbent on 2 GPUs, V4.1-Flash must reach ≥ 130 tok/s C1 and ≥ 3.8K tok/s prefill at 1M; FEASIBILITY's hybrid ceiling (50–78 tok/s) says single-stream will not be won on 2 cards — the 2-GPU case is quality/context, the 3-/4-GPU columns are where it competes on speed.

## E19 — First V4.1-Flash serve on two GPUs: what does the three-tier hybrid actually do? (GPU; T3.6d)

- Question: E6-DESIGN sized the 2-GPU hybrid (208 GPU experts + 176 CPU experts per layer, Engram on NVMe via the `file` layout) and FEASIBILITY put its decode ceiling at 50–78 tok/s. Serve it once, measure C1/C4 decode, 16K prefill, correctness at 13K tokens, temperatures, and the CPU/GPU split — and find every crash between "flags accepted" and "first coherent token".
- Hypothesis: it fits (≤ 94 GB/card), decodes at 40–60 tok/s C1, prefill is CPU-bound (≈ 200–300 tok/s) until T3.6b2 lands, and the first blockers are SM120-specific paths nobody has run V4.1 through.
- Procedure: `dsv41-kt:recipe` + overlay bind-mount (command in TROUBLESHOOTING §"First-serve blockers"), `--kt-num-gpu-experts 208 --context-length 32768 --max-running-requests 4 --cuda-graph-backend-decode breakable`; `bench-decode.py --concurrency 1 --max-tokens 256 --runs 3` then `--concurrency 2,4 --distinct-prompts`; `bench-prefill.py --tokens 16384 --runs 2`; `verify.py --context 16384 --needles 3`; `nvidia-smi -l 5` temps through the window (`evidence/t36d-first-serve/temps-*.csv`).
- Pass: a coherent answer, `verify.py` all-pass, and numbers in `evidence/e19-dsv41-first-serve/` — the recipe's first real V4.1 row.
- **Result (2026-09-21/22, attempts 1–6; `evidence/e19-dsv41-first-serve/`, logs in `evidence/t36d-first-serve/`):**
  1. **It serves.** Five fixes to get there (each a TROUBLESHOOTING section): libnvrtc pruning (`c031247`); the SM120 indexer defaults (`SGLANG_OPT_USE_TILELANG_INDEXER=0 SGLANG_FP8_PAGED_MQA_LOGITS_TORCH=0` + `--enable-deepseek-v4-fp4-indexer`); breakable-graph output-type recursion (overlay `cd55166a`); FlashInfer's `sparse_mla_sm120_prefill` only compiling `extra_page_block_size ∈ {64, 2}` while SGLang pages the ratio-2 pool at 128 → re-page to 64 through `_split_kv_pages_to_64(..., buffer_tag="extra")` (overlay `cd55166a`, costs ≈ 2–3 GB/card); a NaN device-side assert (item 5).
  2. **Footprint:** load_weight 96.8 s, scheduler e2e 189 s, decode graph capture 16.3 s (bs 1/2/4), 93.1 GB/card after load, 7.05 GB "available" pool, `max_total_num_tokens` 3.42 M at 32K context, prefill CUDA graph auto-disabled by the engine for DSv4 + breakable.
  3. **Decode C1: 13.98 tok/s** median (13.91–14.02; TTFT 1.11 s; 8 distinct prompts alone: 13.8–14.1). C4 same-prompt 87.3 aggregate / 72.0 wall; **C4 distinct-prompt 86.7 aggregate / 75.8 wall** (256 tok, attempt 6); C2 distinct 28.0 / 26.1; C3 distinct 64.0 (64 tok). `--distinct-prompts` (new in `bench-decode.py`) was added because same-prompt streams route to the same experts and the CPU tier reads each once — but distinct routing (≈ 4× the expert bytes) costs **nothing** at bs=4. That is the cleanest evidence the CPU tier is not read-bound and the step is fixed overhead.
  4. **Prefill: 215.6 tok/s** at 16,020 tokens (TTFT 75 s; ≈ 17 s per 4096 chunk at 235–252 tok/s; GPUs at 90–115 W). The image's `kt_ep_wrapper.py` has no GPU-side prefill for CPU-resident experts (only `--kt-max-deferred-experts-per-token`), so prefill runs the 176 CPU experts per layer on 16 cores. T3.6b2 (the fork's `_Mxfp4LayerwisePrefillManager`) is the fix.
  5. **Correctness:** `verify.py` at 16K **all pass** — arithmetic, thinking toggle, tool call, 3/3 needles at 13,463 prompt tokens (TTFT 57 s). First answer ("Paris… Seine") coherent.
  6. **Where the time goes:** per-step ≈ 71 ms at bs=1 with the GPUs at 144/159 W and rank 0's process at ≈ 1,880 % CPU vs rank 1's 120 % — CPU experts and Engram lookups run on TP rank 0 only (`if self.tp_rank == 0` in `kt_ep_wrapper.py`). C4 per-stream (21.8) > C1 (14.0) even with shared routing, so the bs=1 step is fixed per-step overhead (2 Engram graph breaks + per-layer CPU sync), not bandwidth. GPU1 shows *higher* nvidia-smi utilization (97 % vs 46 %) because its NCCL all-reduce spin kernel is resident while it waits — not more work.
  7. **Temps (Max-Q, 300 W cap, 3 new case fans):** idle 40/42 °C at 14/28 W; loaded-idle 56/53 °C at 72/77 W; C1 decode 69/67 °C; C4 decode 73/72 °C at 172/189 W; prefill 65/64 °C. Nothing near the 88 °C GLM prefill saw — the hybrid never pushes the GPUs.
  8. **Open blocker (attempt 5, 12 min in):** `probability tensor contains either inf, nan or element < 0` on the first decode after a 4-sequence distinct-prompt prefill; async error surfaced in `engram.py:784 _engram_file_rows`. **Not deterministic:** attempt 6 passed every prompt alone, C3 and C4 distinct at 64 tokens, and (see readout) the exact original c2→c4/256-token sequence. Suspects, in order: (a) stale/garbage rows in the re-paged extra pool being read past a row's compressed length by the SM120 prefill kernel when pool pages are reused, (b) padding (`-1` indices / `topk_length`) for the shorter rows of a mixed batch in the decode kernel with runtime `pbs_extra` 128/256, (c) the CPU expert path at bs≥2 with distinct routing. Next probe: zero-fill the split buffer on allocation and run a 30-min distinct-prompt soak; if it still fires, `SGLANG_SM120_FLASHMLA_BACKEND=triton` as a cross-check.
- Verdict vs FEASIBILITY: 14 tok/s C1 is 3.5–5.5× *below* the 50–78 tok/s ceiling, and the shape of the data says why — the ceiling assumed bandwidth-bound expert reads; the step is overhead-bound. E14 targets (≥ 130 C1, ≥ 3.8K prefill) are out of reach on this layout without T3.6b2 (prefill) and a per-step-overhead attack (decode): fewer graph breaks (A-full Engram prefetch, upstream #40222-style logits-out-of-graph), splitting CPU expert dispatch across both ranks, and `--kt-num-gpu-experts` as high as the ≈ 4 GB left after the split buffers allows (224 probably does not fit).

## E20 — The dense FP8 GEMM, not the experts, owned the V4.1 decode step: tune the Triton W8A8 block-[32,32] kernel for SM120 (GPU; T3.6d3)

- Question: T3.6d3's bs=1 step profile (`evidence/t36d3-step-profile/prof-bs1`, 8 steps) put 42.9 of 70 ms in 202 calls/step of SGLang's Triton `_w8a8_block_fp8_matmul` — the *dense* FP8 GEMMs (attention projections, shared expert, dense-layer MLPs; block scales 32×32), not the offloaded experts. Startup had logged `Using default W8A8 Block FP8 kernel config` 14 times (attempt 6). Is the default config the reason, and how much of the step comes back if the seven shapes are tuned for this card?
- Hypothesis: yes — upstream ships `block_shape=[32, 32]` configs only for H200; the default (BLOCK_M 64 / N 32 / K 32, 3 stages) runs a 1×N GEMV at 60–80 GB/s on a 1,600 GB/s card. A per-shape, per-M search over tiles, warps, stages, `SPLIT_K` and `SWAP_AB` should get 5–10× at M=1 and roughly halve the step.
- Procedure: `kernels/e20/tune_w8a8_block32.py` (own tuner — the stock one has an empty search space at `block_k=32`, because BLOCK_K must divide the 32-wide scale block) run inside `dsv41-kt` on GPU1 beside the serving GPU0 (`--rep 30`, pass 1 = full space at M ∈ {1,2,3,4,8,…,4096}; pass 2 = split-K space for M ≤ 128; `kernels/e20/merge_configs.py` takes the per-M minimum); every winner checked against a chunked FP32 reference (rtol/atol 2e-2). Seven JSONs committed to the overlay (`32a25f37`) under the card's device name, plus the `fp8_kernel.py` gate lifted to `is_sm90 or is_sm120` so `SPLIT_K`/`SWAP_AB` configs are honoured. Relaunch via `scripts/serve-hybrid.sh` (new; attempt-5 flags, overlay bind-mounted); confirm 7× `Using configuration from …` and 0 warnings; `verify.py --context 16384 --needles 3`; `bench-decode.py` C1 ×3 and C4 `--distinct-prompts`; then `kernels/e20/check_sglang_w8a8_path.py` (the real SGLang entry point at M ∈ {1,2,3,4,5,7,768,769,1024,1025} for all seven shapes vs FP32).
- Pass: warnings gone, verify all-pass, C1 ≥ 25 tok/s (the projection was ≈ 31), and the C4-distinct run that crashed attempt 5 survives.
- **Result (2026-09-22; `evidence/e20-dense-fp8-gemm/`):**
  1. **Kernel, M=1 (µs default → tuned):** 16384×1280 371 → 30.7 (12.1×); 1792×5120 119 → 14.3 (8.3×); 2304×5120 150 → 16.4 (9.1×); 5120×1152 115 → 12.3 (9.3×); 5120×4096 258 → 30.7 (8.4×); 4096×1280 102 → 12.3; 25600×6144 1,220 → 217 (5.6×). That is 650–750 GB/s of a 1,600 GB/s roofline, with 12–14 µs as the launch floor. M=4: 2.7–5.6×; M=32–128 fixed by the split-K pass (1792×5120 M=32 68 → 27 µs); M=4096 only 2.2–3.0× and ≈ 230 TFLOPS (≈ 15 % of FP8 peak) — prefill wants a CUTLASS/DeepGEMM block-scaled kernel, not more Triton tuning. Winners: BLOCK_M 16, BLOCK_N 32–128, 2 warps, 4–5 stages, SPLIT_K 8 when K ≥ 4096, SWAP_AB at mid M — the H200 file's answer for M=1 (SWAP_AB + SPLIT_K 8) generalises.
  2. **Server:** 7/7 `Using configuration from …`, 0 default warnings (attempt 6 had 14). `verify.py` at 16K **all pass** (arithmetic, thinking toggle, tool call, 3/3 needles at 13,463 tokens, TTFT 74 s). **C1 decode 31.60 tok/s** median (31.42–31.75, 3 runs, 256 tok, TTFT 1.07 s) vs 13.98 in E19 — **2.26×**, exactly the 42.9 → ≈ 4.5 ms/step projection (≈ 32 ms/step). Scheduler log during decode: 30.4–32.6 tok/s per step.
  3. **C4 distinct (attempt 7): crashed** on the first decode after a mixed prefill (1 running + 3 new × 256 tokens) with `probability tensor contains either inf, nan or element < 0` on *both* TP ranks — the E19 item-8 blocker, third sighting (attempts 5, 7 fail; 6 passed the same pattern three times). Log: `evidence/t36d-first-serve/attempt7/`. GPU0 was left at "100 %, 2 MiB, 95 W" (dead context; cleared by the next process).
  4. **Tuned kernels ruled out as the NaN source:** `check_sglang_w8a8_path.py` — 70/70 cases finite, max relative error 0.0037–0.0043 (bf16 rounding) at every M including the untuned 3, 5, 7, 769, 1,025 (`check-sglang-path-mixed-M.out`).
  5. **T3.6d2 probe applied (overlay `1d6a98d3`):** the persistent page-split output buffer in `flash_mla_sm120.py::_split_kv_pages_to_64` was `torch.empty`; destination pages the touched-page mask never marks keep stale VRAM bytes, and in FP8 e4m3 `0x7F`/`0xFF` are NaN — a read of an unmarked page (padding rows of a mixed batch) poisons the logits with a probability that depends on what last lived in that memory, which fits "non-deterministic, first mixed batch, both ranks". Now `torch.zeros`. Attempt 8 relaunched with it; C4-distinct + soak result recorded below when the window finishes.
  6. **Attempt 8 (zero-filled split buffer): soak passed, C2 is the odd one out.** 02:24–02:59 UTC, 8 blocks × 12 runs × 512 tok on distinct prompts (C4 at fixture offsets 0/8/12/4, C2 at 4/0/8, C3 at 2), 96 mixed batches, **zero NaN** (`evidence/t36d-first-serve/attempt8-soak/`). Aggregate decode: C4 120–122 tok/s (110–115 wall), C3 90.8, **C2 only 39.1–39.9 (≈ 19–20 tok/s per stream)**; C4-distinct 256-tok bench 120.0 agg / 88.0 wall. Scheduler log over the whole window (1,281 `Decode batch` lines): running=1 → 26.7–32.4 tok/s; **running=2 → 19.1 tok/s per stream (≈ 52 ms/step)**; running=3 → 29.2; running=4 → 29.0 per stream (≈ 34 ms/step, same as bs=1). Greedy 12-token logprob check (`/tmp/lp.py`, same prompt alone vs inside a 4-batch): identical tokens, max |Δlogp| 0.011 (run-to-run noise at bs=1 is 0.062) — the CPU experts are computed correctly at bs=4. In the 2-batch, token 6 flipped on a near tie (0.47 vs 0.69 logprob shift, 3× the noise) — worth re-checking once bs=2 is fast.
  7. **Step profiles bs=1 / bs=2 / bs=4 (8 steps each, `scripts/step-profile.py` + `scripts/gap-context.py`, `evidence/e20-dense-fp8-gemm/prof-e20-bs{1,2,4}/`, `step-profile-*.txt`, `gap-context-bs1-bs2-bs4-tp0.txt`):** span 34.7 / 57.6 / 38.7 ms per step; GPU busy 12.3 / 13.3 / 15.7; GPU-idle ≥100 µs **20.1 / 44.3 / 20.1 ms**. Dense FP8 GEMM is now 3.4 ms/step (was 42.9). The idle time is one gap per MoE layer sitting between the GPU experts' last cutlass grouped GEMM and the `Memcpy HtoD (Pinned → Device)` that brings kt-kernel's CPU-expert output back — i.e. the wait on the CPU tier through the `cudaLaunchHostFunc` sync callback: 40 waits/step, **median 470 / 813 / 407 µs at bs=1 / 2 / 4** (17.1 / 34.0 / 15.2 ms per step), quantised in ≈ 0.2 ms steps (≈ one CPU expert each). Two Engram graph breaks per step (`Memcpy DtoH (Device → Pageable)` → `HtoD (Pageable → Device)`, 1.2–1.5 ms each) cost another 2.4–3.0 ms. Plus one-off 10–25 ms stalls (bs=2: 25.1 + 10.3 ms in 8 steps; bs=4: 12.9 ms). The Engram `.to("cpu")` frame that the earlier profile blamed only *absorbs* the wait; the gap-context tool attributes it to the kt H2D copy.
  8. **kt-kernel in isolation does not predict this (E21 harness, `evidence/e21-kt-fixed-floor/`):** with the hybrid's real split (208 GPU experts) `scripts/test-kt-one-layer.py --cpu-slots-per-token {0,1,3,6}` gives a 0.04 ms floor and a clean **0.19 ms per (token × CPU expert)**, linear in tokens (T=1/2/3/4/8 at 3 CPU slots: 0.59 / 1.13 / 1.62 / 2.11 / 4.12 ms). `--shared-routing` (all tokens pick the same experts) still grows 0.3 ms per extra token (T=4 1.50 ms) and a hot-L3 rerun barely helps for T ≥ 2 (T=4 1.92 ms): **T=1 is DRAM-bound (natural-order GEMV fast path), T ≥ 2 is compute-bound in kt's generic per-token GEMV** (`qlen > 1 → forward_prefill`, `operators/amx/fp4-moe.hpp` — the 4×4 mat-mat tile only engages at qlen > 4·384/6 = 256). Isolation predicts ≈ 0.5 / 1.0 / 2.0 ms per layer at bs=1/2/4; the serve shows 0.47 / 0.81 / 0.41. bs=1 and bs=2 fit; **bs=4 is 4–5× faster than kt's own compute cost**, which is unexplained.
  9. **The CPU is oversubscribed (`scripts/kt-cpu-per-step.sh`, `evidence/e21-kt-fixed-floor/kt-cpu-per-step.txt`):** during decode all 16 kt threads (`numa_0_m_0`, `numa_0_t_1..15`) burn 100 % CPU continuously — 115 CPU-s in a 7.2 s C1 run (15.95 threads busy), regardless of batch size: the pool spin-waits between tasks. The TP0 main thread also polls at ≈ 100 % (`cudaStreamIsCapturing`/`cudaEventQuery` under the gaps), TP1 likewise, and the Engram file gather has its own 16 threads. `vmstat` shows **18–21 runnable threads on 16 physical cores** (run queue), so the GPU-launching threads, the CUDA callback thread (`cuda-EvtHandlr`) and kt's compute share SMT siblings. Swap traffic during decode: none (the 4.8 GB in swap is cold).
- Verdict: the step was never expert-bound — the "it works on H100" default Triton config was 57 % of it. With it fixed, C1 sits at 31.6 tok/s (soak-clean at C2–C4, 120 tok/s aggregate at C4) and the next ≈ 20 ms/step is 40 per-layer waits on kt-kernel's CPU tier (17 ms, quantised ≈ 0.2 ms = one CPU expert) plus 2 Engram graph breaks (2.4–3 ms). The waits are not kt compute (isolation says 0.5 ms/layer and bs=4 beats it) but scheduling: 16 spinning kt threads + 2 polling TP threads + CUDA callback on 16 physical cores. That is E21's lever (fewer kt threads, then pinning); T3.6d3's other levers (A-full Engram prefetch, logits-out-of-graph, split CPU-expert dispatch across ranks) stay queued. Prefill's dense GEMMs at M=4096 need a real block-scaled CUTLASS kernel (future K item); Triton tops out at 15 % of FP8 peak there.

## E21 — Where does the fixed ≈ 0.45 ms per-layer CPU-tier wait come from, and does the batch-size dependence go away with fewer kt threads?

- Status: **in progress** 2026-09-22 (GPU window). Owner T3.6d5.
- Question: E20 items 7–9 show the V4.1 decode step is now ≈ 60 % GPU-idle waiting on kt-kernel's CPU experts (40 waits/step, median 470 µs at bs=1, 813 at bs=2, 407 at bs=4), yet kt-kernel in isolation costs 0.5 / 1.0 / 2.0 ms per layer at those batch sizes and the host has 18–21 runnable threads on 16 physical cores. Is the wait (a) kt compute, (b) OS scheduling of oversubscribed spin-waiting threads, or (c) something in the SGLang↔kt handshake (host callback, pinned copy)?
- Hypothesis: (b). 16 spinning kt worker threads + 2 polling TP threads + `cuda-EvtHandlr` + Engram gather threads on 16 physical cores means every per-layer handshake risks a scheduler quantum (≈ 0.2–1 ms) whenever the TP0 main thread or the callback thread is descheduled; bs=2 is the worst because its CPU work spans two quanta. Fewer kt threads (12) should leave physical cores for the launch/callback threads and remove most of the fixed floor; per-layer kt compute should rise only by 16/12 (0.5 → 0.67 ms at bs=1 in isolation) — the fixed term should fall more than the compute term rises.
- Procedure:
  1. Isolation baseline (done before the relaunch, `evidence/e21-kt-fixed-floor/layer5-gpu208-cpuslots{0,1,3,6}.json`, `*-shared-cold.json`, `*-hot.json`): `scripts/test-kt-one-layer.py --num-gpu-experts 208 --skip-reference --bench-cold --cpu-slots-per-token N` — see E20 item 8.
  2. Host CPU census at C1/2/4 (done, `kt-cpu-per-step.txt`, `vmstat-during-kt-cpu.txt`, `scripts/kt-cpu-per-step.sh`) — see E20 item 9.
  3. Relaunch `KT_CPUINFER=12 bash scripts/serve-hybrid.sh` (everything else attempt-8 defaults: 208 GPU experts, graphs bs [1,2,4], 32K ctx, `ENGRAM_FILE_THREADS=16`), `bench-decode.py --concurrency 1,2,4 --max-tokens 256 --runs 3 --warmup 1 --distinct-prompts` → `cpuinfer12.json`; rerun `kt-cpu-per-step.sh`; profile 8 steps at bs=2 and analyse with `step-profile.py --compare` + `gap-context.py`.
  4. If the floor moves: sweep `KT_CPUINFER` 14 and 8; then `--cpuset-cpus`/`taskset` pinning of kt threads to physical cores 0–11 with TP threads on 12–15 (SMT siblings 16–31 left to Engram gather).
  5. If the floor does not move: per-thread run-queue wait from `/proc/<tid>/schedstat` (`perf` needs root here: `perf_event_paranoid=4`) — new `scripts/sched-wait-per-step.py` snapshots every thread in the container around one bench run and prints run time, run-queue wait and voluntary/involuntary switches per decode step.
  6. Hide the wait instead of shrinking it: relaunch with `EXTRA_SERVER_ARGS="--kt-max-deferred-experts-per-token D"` (kt-kernel's expert deferral: the D lowest-scored routed experts of each token are computed during the *next* layer and their output is accumulated one layer late — `experts_base.py::_prepare_forward_cpu_buffers` → `select_deferred_experts`, `submit_forward` submits a second `deferred_task` into `output_cpu[next_slot]`, the next layer's `immediate_task` runs `incremental=True`; the last MoE layer always uses 0). Bench C1/2/4 distinct, then quality: `scripts/verify.py` at 16K + `/tmp/lp.py` greedy logprobs vs the D=0 run. D=2 first, then D=4 and D=6 (all-deferred) if quality holds.
- Pass criteria: bs=1 per-layer wait median < 300 µs (from 470) **and** bs=2 per-stream ≥ bs=1 × 0.85 (from 0.6×) at C1 ≥ 31 tok/s; no NaN in the 3×3 distinct-prompt bench. For deferral additionally: verify.py all-pass and greedy 12-token output unchanged on the `/tmp/lp.py` prompt (|Δlogp| within 3× the 0.06 run-to-run noise).
- Result (2026-09-22, GPU window):
  1. **`KT_CPUINFER=12`: nothing moves.** C1 31.16 (31.07–31.27), C2 36.62, C4 120.37 tok/s aggregate (`cpuinfer12.json`) vs attempt 8's 31.6 / ≈ 39 / 120. `kt-cpu-per-step.sh` (`kt-cpu-per-step-cpuinfer12.txt`): 11.1–11.8 kt threads busy (the 12 spin as the 16 did), kt CPU-s/step 0.44 / 0.74 / 0.52 at C1/2/4 (was 0.58 / 0.93 / 0.67 — just fewer spinners). `vmstat` run queue 14–17 (was 18–21), 47 % user / 52 % idle on 32 logical CPUs.
  2. **Run-queue wait is negligible — the scheduling hypothesis is false.** `sched-wait-per-step.py` at C1/2/4 (`sched-wait-cpuinfer12-c{1,2,4}.txt`): kt workers 0.21 / 0.03 / 0.06 ms run-queue wait **per step** (12 threads summed; vs 17–34 ms of GPU idle per step), TP main threads 0.22 / 0.02 / 0.06 ms, involuntary context switches ≤ 1.1 per step for the whole kt pool and ≤ 0.5 for the TP main threads. `cuda-EvtHandlr` wakes 183–185×/step (voluntary) with 0.001–0.008 ms of queue wait. Nothing that the GPU waits on is waiting for a core.
  3. **So the per-layer wait is kt's own time, and at bs=1 it is a DRAM-latency figure:** 0.19 ms per CPU expert = 21.7 MiB / 0.19 ms ≈ 120 GB/s, one expert at a time (the ≈ 0.2 ms quantisation of the gaps), 2.75 CPU experts expected per token at 208/384 GPU placement → 0.5 ms/layer → 19–20 ms of the 35 ms step. That is 30 % of the 409 GB/s the 8 DDR5-6400 channels can deliver — the m=1 GEMV fast path is latency/parallelism-bound, not bandwidth-bound. bs=2 (0.81 ms, 5.5 slots) matches the same rate; bs=4 (0.41 ms for 11 slots) does not — the bs=4 anomaly stays open (the bs=4 profile *was* a distinct-prompt run, 8 fixtures).
  4. **Deferral had never worked on this recipe.** The first `--kt-max-deferred-experts-per-token 2` launch died in the dummy forward with `ScatterGatherKernel.cu: scatter gather kernel index out of bounds`: kt's `select_deferred_experts` scatters the protected expert ids into a `[num_experts]` flag tensor, and the compact CPU view passes `-1` for GPU-resident slots. Fixed in the overlay (`11e7f745`, `kt_ep_wrapper.py::_select_deferred_experts_compact`, bound onto the wrapper when D > 0): same batch-level rule as upstream, `-1` routed to a spare flag slot and masked back to `-1` on both outputs, no data-dependent shapes (graph-capturable). Checked inside the image against upstream's function on 16 random (bs, D) cases: identical; `-1` slots stay `-1` in both outputs.
  5. **D=2 (`KT_CPUINFER=16`): C1 35.22 (35.10–35.30), C2 43.77, C4 131.49 tok/s aggregate** (`deferred2.json`) — **+11 % / +12 % / +9 %** over D=0 (31.6 / 39 / 120). Matches the model: 2 of 6 slots deferred hides ≈ ⅓ of the 17 ms wait → step 35 → 29–30 ms. Prefill benefits too: 13.5K-token needle TTFT 61.3 s (was 74 s). `verify.py` at 16K **ALL PASS** (arithmetic, thinking toggle, tool call, 3/3 needles; `verify-16k-d2.*`).
  6. **D=2 output is batch-size dependent where D=0 was not.** `scripts/greedy-logprobs.py` (4 prompts, 32 greedy tokens, `greedy-d2.json` vs `greedy-d2-bs4.json`): the same prompt inside a 4-batch diverges from its bs=1 answer on all 4 prompts at tokens 7–15, max |Δlogp| 0.17–0.63 on the common prefix (bs=1 run-to-run noise 0.06; at D=0 bs=4 was token-identical with max Δ 0.011). Part of this is inherent — the "protected for any token in the batch" rule makes *which* experts are deferred depend on the batch-mates — but the size needs the D=0 baseline (`greedy-d0.json`, next relaunch) before calling it acceptable.
  7. **Greedy-token divergence is noise on this hybrid, so item 6 was measuring the wrong thing.** D=0 relaunch, `greedy-logprobs.py` (`greedy-d0.json`, `greedy-d0-rerun.json`, `greedy-d0-bs4.json`): two bs=1 runs of the *same* D=0 server already pick different tokens on 2/4 prompts (Rome at token 1 — ` Western` vs ` fall`; Fibonacci at token 12), and bs=4 vs bs=1 diverges at token 0–1 on 3/4 prompts (`From` vs `Let`). Max |Δlogp| on the common prefix ≤ 0.32 at D=0 vs D=0, ≤ 0.26 for D=2 vs D=0 — the D=2 drift is inside the D=0 run-to-run band. The flips are at near-ties (two candidates within ≈ 0.1 nats) and the decode path is not bit-reproducible (kt thread-order and MoE reduction order both vary), so "diverges at token N" carries no information about deferral quality. E20's "0.06 noise" came from one prompt with no near-tie in 12 tokens.
  8. **Replacement metric: teacher-forced NLL** (`scripts/nll-score.py`: SGLang native `/generate` with `return_logprob`, `logprob_start_len=0`, scoring the frozen `scripts/fixtures/nll-corpus.txt`, 4,795 tokens). D=0: **1.48741 nats/token** (ppl 4.426), per-1K segments 2.034 / 1.924 / 1.189 / 1.056 / 1.117; a rerun is **bit-identical** (ΔNLL 0.00000, max |Δlogp| 0.000 — the prefill path is deterministic, `nll-d0{,-rerun}.json`). Deferral is applied in `submit_forward` for every forward, prefill included (no decode gating in `_prepare_forward_cpu_buffers`), so this scores the same approximation the decode path uses.
  9. **D=4: C1 38.53 (38.50–39.09), C2 45.44, C4 146.96 tok/s aggregate** (`deferred4.json`) — **+22 % / +17 % / +22 %** over D=0 and +9 % / +4 % / +12 % over D=2. Needle TTFT 57.9 s (D=2 61.3, D=0 74). `verify.py` 16K **ALL PASS** (`verify-16k-d4.*`). At 208/384 GPU experts a token routes ≈ 2.75 of its 6 slots to the CPU; with `protected_k = 2` only CPU experts that land in a token's top-2 are computed on time, so D=4 defers most of the CPU work — hence the larger step than D=2.
  10. **D=4 quality: mean NLL 1.48132 vs 1.48741 (ΔNLL −0.0061 nats/token, z = +1.44 over 4,795 paired tokens — not significant), but the perturbation per token is real and large:** mean |Δlogp| 0.132, sd 0.293, 293 tokens move by > 0.5 nats — **144 worse, 149 better**, 3 vs 5 beyond 2 nats (`nll-d4.json`; rerun bit-identical, `nll-d4-rerun.json`). Deferral behaves as zero-mean per-token noise on the residual stream, not as a systematic loss, on this 4.8K-token text. That is the same signature quantisation shows; it is not free, but it did not cost NLL here.
  11. **D=6 (every CPU expert one layer late, `protected_k = 0`) is broken on both axes.** NLL **1.71876 vs 1.48741 — ΔNLL +0.231 nats/token, z = −15**, mean |Δlogp| 0.50, sd 1.07, 982 tokens worse vs 456 better by > 0.5 nats, max 13.7 (`nll-d6.json`). The decode bench then killed the server: `TensorCompare.cu … probability tensor contains either inf, nan or element < 0` device-side assert in the sampler during the decode bench (container log quoted here; the container was removed for the next relaunch), i.e. NaN logits. Not investigated further — the quality number alone disqualifies it. The top-scored expert of each token must be on time.
  12. **D=2 NLL: 1.48662 — ΔNLL −0.0008 nats/token, z = +0.21**, mean |Δlogp| 0.123, sd 0.262, 133 worse / 134 better by > 0.5 nats (`nll-d2.json`). Same zero-mean signature as D=4 and barely smaller per token: what matters is that a token's *top* expert is on time, not how many of the tail are late.
  13. **D=5 (`protected_k = 1`): NLL-neutral but unstable at bs=4.** NLL 1.48754 — ΔNLL +0.0001, z = −0.03, mean |Δlogp| 0.142, sd 0.292, 181 worse / 164 better (`nll-d5.json`). The bench ran C1 and C2 cleanly — server-side `gen throughput` medians 40.45 (bs=1, n=25) and 46.13 tok/s (bs=2, n=26), so roughly +3–5 % over D=4 — then the **first bs=4 decode step** hit the same `probability tensor contains inf/nan` sampler assert and killed the server (`serve-d5-crash.log`, ASGI shutdown noise trimmed). So the deferral NaN is batch-size dependent: D=5 dies at bs=4 (D=6 died somewhere in the same C1→C4 bench; its log was not kept), D=4 has not died (3 × 3 runs + verify at bs ≤ 4; longer soak below). Where the NaN comes from (kt's deferred-task buffer at `protected_k ≤ 1`, or an all-`-1` immediate task for a whole batch) is an open item, not chased here: D=5's ≈ 4 % is not worth it and D=6's quality is gone regardless.
  14. **Default set: `KT_MAX_DEFERRED=4`** in `scripts/serve-hybrid.sh` (`--kt-max-deferred-experts-per-token`; `KT_MAX_DEFERRED=0` disables). Soak on the default launch, distinct prompts, 512 tokens, 5 runs + warm-up: **C4 146.86 (145.0–148.7), C3 111.71 (108.1–112.2) tok/s aggregate, no NaN** over ≈ 21K generated tokens at bs ≥ 3 (`soak-d4-default.json`). bs=3 runs 37.2 per stream — almost linear from bs=1 — while bs=2 stays at 22.7 per stream, so the bs=2 dip is now the odd point, not bs=4. NLL on the default launch is bit-identical to `nll-d4.json` (`nll-d4-default.json`).
- Verdict: **pass, by a different route than planned.** The wait was never scheduling (hypothesis falsified in result 2) and it did not shrink — it is hidden: D=4 overlaps most of the CPU tier with the next layer's GPU work. Speed: C1 31.6 → **38.5** (+22 %), C2 39 → 45.4, C4 120 → **147**; 13.5K needle TTFT 74 → 58 s. Quality: ΔNLL −0.006 nats/token (z 1.4), 16K verify all-pass, D=0-vs-D=0 greedy flips shown to be noise. Not met: bs=2 per-stream ≥ 0.85× bs=1 (0.59×) — carried with the bs=4 anomaly into T3.6d3. Open: D=5/D=6 sampler NaN (batch-size dependent; D=5 would be worth ≈ 4 %), bs=2 dip, Engram graph breaks.

## E22 — Layerwise GPU prefill for the CPU-resident experts: what does re-exporting the CPU tier cost, and how fast can prefill get?

- Status: **in progress** 2026-09-23 (GPU window). Owner T3.6b2.
- Question: prefill is 216 tok/s because the 176 CPU experts per layer run on 16 cores for every 4,096-token chunk (E19 item 4). The fork's `_Mxfp4LayerwisePrefillManager` instead streams each layer's CPU experts to the GPUs and runs the MoE there. On SM120 our GPU tier (`Mxfp4FlashinferCutlassMoEMethod`) consumes the checkpoint's raw MXFP4 bytes, so the streamed experts need no Marlin repack — only a gate/up half swap (kt writes `[gate; up]`, SGLang stores `[up; gate]`), a bf16→E8M0 scale shift and FlashInfer's `block_scale_interleave`. What bounds the streamed path: kt-kernel's host-side export (`write_weight_scale_to_buffer`, a multithreaded memcpy of ≈ 20 MB per expert, 3.5 GB per layer, 113 GiB per chunk), the two x16 links (E4: 53 GiB/s each → 1.06 s per chunk), or the GPU GEMMs?
